People take everything so literally these days, don't they? \n\nAnyway, I haven't been keeping up with them, or my kayaking plans either. Which is a shame, honestly, because I have this beautiful yellow fiberglass kayak. Fiberglass glides through the water so much better than the cheap plastic ones, though if you hit a rock, you really feel it. The river by my house is full of these big granite rocks, and the quartz in them sparkles right around 4 PM when the sun hits the water... but yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right. I just don't do any of those things much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have persistent, unwanted thoughts or images that come into your mind that seem to be connected to these tapping patterns or freezing episodes?\n\nPatient: Thoughts or images? No, not connected to the tapping or anything. My mind is mostly just... quiet, I guess, when that happens. \n\nThough, speaking of images getting stuck in your head, lately I keep picturing this old billboard that used to be on Interstate 95. It was for a personal injury lawyer, one of those guys pointing right at you, looking really aggressive. But the paint was peeling right on the guy's nose, so it looked like he had a terrible sunburn. Sunburns are the worst, especially on the top of your shoulders. I fell asleep at the beach once back in the late nineties, just completely passed out on my towel. It was one of those oversized beach towels, the kind with a big dolphin printed on it. \n\nDolphins are incredibly smart, you know. I read they actually have names for each other. They use these specific whistles to call their friends, which is just fascinating. Imagine if people communicated like that at the store. Just whistling over the intercom instead of calling for a price check on aisle four. The intercom at my store has this awful static buzz anyway. It sounds like a hornet's nest every time someone presses the button. But, uh... what was the question? Something about the tapping? Yeah, no, no unwanted thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to complete these tapping patterns or perform certain actions a specific way to prevent something bad from happening or to make things feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, no, it's nothing like that. It's just... I just tap. There's no reason, really. It doesn't make anything feel right or wrong. \n\nSpeaking of right and wrong, did you see they changed the layout of the cereal aisle at the grocery store? I went in there on Tuesday—or maybe it was Wednesday, because it was raining, and my umbrella has this broken spoke that always catches in my hair. It's a black umbrella with a wooden handle. I bought it at a hardware store years ago. You don't see wooden handles much anymore, do you? Everything is plastic or that rubbery material that gets sticky if you leave it in a hot car. \n\nI left a cassette tape in my car once back in, gosh, '92? It was a Fleetwood Mac album, and it completely warped in the sun. Sounded like they were singing underwater. Water always distorts sound so strangely. If you ever put your head under the bathwater, you can hear the pipes clanking in the walls. Our house had copper pipes, which I guess are valuable now because people keep stealing them out of abandoned buildings. Scrap metal prices fluctuate so much, it's basically like the stock market. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... no, the tapping isn't to stop anything bad from happening. I don't feel like I *have* to do it for any particular reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing any other repetitive actions or rituals, either physically or in your mind, that are similar to the tapping pattern in any way?\n\nPatient: Other repetitive actions? No, nothing like that. Just the tapping. \n\nThough, you know, speaking of repetitive things, they play the exact same twelve songs on a loop at the store where I work. Twelve songs. I counted them once during a slow shift. It’s always that one soft rock station, the kind that plays Phil Collins and, you know, maybe some old Elton John. Elton John has so many glasses, doesn't he? I saw a documentary once that said he owns thousands of pairs of sunglasses. I only have one pair, and they're scratched right down the middle of the left lens. I dropped them in a parking lot last winter when I was scraping ice off my windshield. \n\nIce scrapers are so poorly designed. You always end up with snow inside your sleeves, right around the wrists. It’s the worst feeling, cold wet cuffs. My grandmother used to knit these thick wool sweaters that were so warm, but they were incredibly itchy. She always used this dark green yarn that smelled faintly of mothballs. Mothballs are just pure chemicals, aren't they? I don't even know if people use them anymore, now that everyone has cedar closets. Cedar smells wonderful, like a forest after it rains. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, no other rituals or anything. Just the tapping.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any sudden, brief movements or sounds that you couldn't control, like blinking, shrugging, or throat-clearing?\n\nPatient: Sudden movements? No, I can control my blinking and all that. I don't have any twitches. I mean, I clear my throat if I have a tickle, but that's just normal. \n\nThough, speaking of tickles in the throat, the pollen has been absolutely terrible this year. Have you noticed the yellow dust on all the cars in the parking lot? My neighbor has this navy blue sedan, and it's basically green right now. Navy blue is a terrible color for showing dirt. I had a navy blue suit once, bought it for a cousin's wedding back in Ohio. It was an outdoor wedding in late October, which is a massive gamble with the weather if you ask me. \n\nIt ended up being freezing, just this biting wind, and for the dinner they served cold cucumber soup as the starter. Who serves cold soup when it's forty degrees outside? Soup should be hot. Like a really good minestrone. Minestrone always has those dark red kidney beans in it, though, and I'm not a huge fan of beans. The texture is just too mealy, kind of like a baked potato that's been left in the microwave for too long. Microwaves ruin bread, too, you know. It makes the crust all rubbery and impossible to chew. \n\nBut, uh... what did you ask? Shrugging? No, no sudden movements or sounds that I can't control.\n\nDoctor: Before you start tapping or freezing, do you ever feel a strange sensation, like a need or urge that builds up and is relieved by doing the action?\n\nPatient: A strange sensation? Like a build-up? No, I don't feel anything building up. It just happens. \n\nThough, talking about pressure building up, the espresso machine at the café next to my store has been making this awful hissing noise lately. The barista said the steam wand is clogged. Steam is so dangerous, you know. I burned my wrist on a tea kettle once years ago. I was making Earl Grey. Earl Grey always tastes a bit like perfume to me, probably because of the bergamot. Bergamot is a citrus fruit, which is strange because it doesn't look like a lemon or an orange at all. It's green and really bumpy. My aunt used to grow these bumpy decorative gourds in her garden every autumn. She'd put them in a big wooden bowl on the dining table. \n\nWooden bowls are so hard to take care of, you can't put them in the dishwasher or the heat cracks them right down the middle. My brother put a wooden cutting board in the dishwasher once and completely ruined it. He was always doing things like that, just never reading the instructions. Instructions are printed so small these days anyway, you need a magnifying glass just to read how to set the time on a microwave. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Something about an urge? No, no, I don't feel anything like that before I freeze or tap.\n\nDoctor: Before or during these tapping or freezing episodes, do you ever notice any unusual sensory experiences, like strange smells, sounds, or things you see that don't seem to be there?\n\nPatient: Unusual sensory things? Well, I don't know about smells or seeing things, but... well, most days I hear what sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or saying I rang something up wrong. But when I turn around, there's nobody there, and the other cashiers never hear it. \n\nSpeaking of ringing things up wrong, the new point-of-sale system they installed last month is just a nightmare. The touch screen is completely unresponsive if your fingers are even slightly cold. My hands are always cold, poor circulation, I suppose. My grandfather had terrible circulation, he used to wear these thick wool socks even in the middle of July. July in July is unbearable, especially with the humidity. It makes the air feel thick, like you're trying to breathe underwater. I tried scuba diving once on a vacation to Florida back in the nineties. \n\nFlorida is so flat, isn't it? Just miles and miles of strip malls and palm trees. Palm trees aren't actually trees, you know, they're more closely related to grass. It's fascinating how taxonomy works, grouping things together based on cellular structures instead of what they look like. Like how a tomato is a fruit, but everyone treats it like a vegetable. You'd never put a tomato in a fruit salad, that would be disgusting. Fruit salad always has honeydew melon, which is just filler if you ask me. Nobody actually likes honeydew. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right. The voices. Yeah, just those people talking when no one's there. No strange smells or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these tapping patterns or freezing episodes happen only when you're at work, or do they also occur in other situations, like when you're at home or out running errands?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly at work. That's actually why I came in, because at the register I keep getting stuck doing these things that just don't fit the moment. Like, I'll be ringing someone up and I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. It's getting really bad. Last week, it actually got me written up by the manager. \n\nManagers can be so unreasonable, though. He wrote it on this pink slip of paper. Why do they still use pink slips? It feels so outdated, like something out of a fifties movie. I love old movies, especially the ones with Cary Grant. He always wore those incredible tailored suits. Tailoring is a lost art, really. Nowadays, everyone just buys off the rack and expects it to fit perfectly, but human bodies aren't built on an assembly line. \n\nAssembly lines completely changed the automotive industry, Henry Ford and all that. My dad had an old Ford pickup truck when I was a kid. It was this faded cherry red, and the vinyl seats would get so hot in the summer they'd practically blister your legs if you wore shorts. I don't wear shorts anymore, not since I turned forty. I just feel like grown men shouldn't wear shorts unless they're at the beach. The beach is getting so crowded these days, though. You can't even find a spot to put down a towel without being right on top of someone else's umbrella. Umbrellas are tricky in the wind, they always turn inside out. \n\nBut... wait. What were you asking me about? Errands? Oh, no, the freezing and tapping... it really just happens when I'm at the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these voices calling your name or talking to you only when you're at work or during these tapping or freezing episodes, or do you hear them at other times too, like when you're at home or alone?\n\nPatient: Well, the voices... I hear them mostly at work, like I said before. It's usually when I'm near the stockroom, just off to the side. They'll call my name or tell me I messed up ringing something up. But I don't really hear them at home. Home is pretty quiet. \n\nToo quiet, sometimes. I live in this apartment building that used to be an old shoe factory. They converted it back in the early two-thousands. Exposed brick is nice to look at, but it’s terrible for insulation. In the winter, the draft just cuts right through the walls. I bought this heavy velvet curtain to put over the window, thinking it would help block the cold. Velvet is such a strange fabric, isn't it? It catches the dust like crazy. I feel like I'm constantly running the lint roller over it. Lint rollers are basically just giant rolls of tape on a stick. \n\nTape is one of those inventions you don't really think about until you need it and can't find the end of the roll. My dad used to get so frustrated with duct tape, he'd just take a pocket knife and slash a new edge into it. He carried that pocket knife everywhere. It had a little bone handle that was yellowed from age. He said he got it at a hardware store in Chicago when he was a teenager. \n\nChicago is famous for deep-dish pizza, but honestly, I think it's more of a casserole than a pizza. If you have to eat it with a knife and fork, it defeats the purpose of pizza. Pizza should be something you can fold in half and eat while walking down the street in New York. New York always seems so exhausting, though. Everyone is always rushing somewhere. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About the voices? Yeah, like I said, it's just those people talking off to the side, mostly at work. They call my name or say I rang something wrong, but no one's there. I don't really hear them at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or noticed any other unusual sounds or sensations when you're not at work or when you're alone, even when there's no one around to explain it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's mostly at work, you know? Most days I hear these couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or telling me I rang something up wrong. But when I turn around to look, there's nobody there, and the other cashiers never hear a thing. \n\nI don't really hear them when I'm alone at home, though. Home is... well, it's quiet. I have this old grandfather clock in the hallway, and sometimes the ticking is the only sound in the whole apartment. It belonged to my great-aunt Mildred. She was a bit eccentric, used to collect porcelain thimbles. She had hundreds of them, all lined up in these little wooden display cases. I never understood the appeal of collecting things you can't even use. It's just dusting, really. Dusting is such a chore. I tried using one of those microfiber wands, but it just seems to push the dust around instead of picking it up. \n\nMicrofiber is a strange material. It feels weird on your skin, almost like it's catching on your fingerprints. I read somewhere that criminals used to burn off their fingerprints with acid so they wouldn't get caught. Can you imagine the pain? Acid is terrifying. I remember in high school chemistry, Mr. Henderson dropped a beaker of hydrochloric acid and it ate right through the linoleum floor. Linoleum is making a comeback, you know. People are putting it in their kitchens again because it's eco-friendly. \n\nEco-friendly is such a buzzword now. Every product claims to be green, but half of it is just marketing. Like those paper straws—they dissolve in your drink before you're even halfway done! I'd rather just not use a straw at all. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? About the sounds? Yeah, just the voices at work, like I said. Calling my name or talking about the register. But not when I'm alone or at home. Just at the store.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people don't see, like shadows, people, or objects that aren't actually there?\n\nPatient: See things? No, no shadows or anything like that. Like I keep telling you, it's really just the sounds. Most days I just hear what sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or saying I rang something up wrong, but when I turn around there’s nobody there and no one else heard it. \n\nI turned around so fast the other day I actually gave myself a terrible crick in my neck. I thought about going to a chiropractor, but I've seen those videos online where they crack your neck and it sounds like a dry branch snapping. Snapping branches always reminds me of camping. My uncle used to take me camping up in the Adirondacks when I was a kid. We'd have to gather kindling for the fire before it got dark. Fire is so mesmerizing, isn't it? You can just stare into a campfire for hours and not even realize how much time has passed. \n\nTime is funny like that. Staring at screens does the same thing to people nowadays, though. Everyone is always just looking down at their phones, completely ignoring the world around them. My phone battery dies so fast lately, I swear they plan it that way so you have to buy a new one every two years. Planned obsolescence, it's a real economic strategy they teach you in business school. I took an economics class once when I was getting my bachelor's, but the professor just droned on and on. He wore these elbow patches on his tweed jackets. I always wondered if the elbows were actually worn out, or if he bought the jacket with the patches already sewn on. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Shadows? Oh, right. No, I don't see anything that isn't there. Just the people talking at the store, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people seem to think are strange or untrue, but you feel certain they are real?\n\nPatient: Strange beliefs? No, I don't think so. I'm a pretty grounded guy. I mean, I read a lot of history, and history is full of people with strange ideas. Like, did you know people used to think tomatoes were poisonous? They called them \"poison apples\" back in the 1700s because aristocrats would eat them off pewter plates, and the acid in the tomato would leach the lead out of the plate and give them lead poisoning. \n\nLead is so incredibly dangerous, but they used to put it in everything. Paint, gasoline. My apartment building—the old shoe factory I mentioned—when they converted it, they had to do a massive lead abatement before anyone could move in. The contractors were walking around in these full-body white suits, looking like astronauts. \n\nSpace travel just seems absolutely terrifying to me, to be honest. Being stuck in a tiny tin can millions of miles from earth with no fresh air. And the food! It's all freeze-dried and vacuum-sealed. I can't stand vacuum-sealed packaging. Sometimes at the store, customers bring up those vacuum-sealed steaks, and the plastic gets all crinkled up around the barcode so the laser scanner won't read it. I have to stand there and type in the twelve-digit UPC code by hand, and the buttons on my register stick sometimes. It slows down my whole line, and then the people waiting start sighing loudly and looking at their watches. \n\nWatches are kind of obsolete now anyway, aren't they? Everyone just uses their phone to check the time. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About strange ideas? Yeah, no. I don't have any weird beliefs or anything like that. Just regular thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or other sounds when there was no one around and you weren’t at work, like when you're alone in your apartment or walking by yourself?\n\nPatient: I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like I was saying before, it's just at the store. Most days I'll be ringing people up and I hear these couple of people talking, just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or telling me I rang something up wrong, but when I turn around there's nobody there. And none of the other cashiers ever hear it. \n\nI really don't hear them when I'm alone in my apartment. My apartment is in that old shoe factory, remember? The walls are incredibly thick. You know, they used to build things to last back in the day. The bricks they used for that factory were probably fired in a local kiln. Brickmaking used to be a huge industry in this area before everything got outsourced. Outsourcing really gutted the manufacturing sector. I was reading this article about how the rust belt got its name, and it's fascinating how entire cities just rusted away when the steel mills closed. \n\nSteel is an incredible alloy, though. Did you know they use a specific type of stainless steel for surgical instruments? It has to be completely non-porous so bacteria can't hide in it. Bacteria are everywhere, though. Like on money. Working a register, you handle so much cash, and cash is filthy. People pull crumpled, damp bills out of their socks or their bras, and I have to touch it. It's so unhygienic. I go through so much hand sanitizer on my shift that my knuckles actually crack and bleed in the winter. Winter here is brutal, the wind just whips right off the lake.\n\nBut yeah, anyway. To answer your question again, no, I don't hear them when I'm alone or walking by myself. Just at work, like I said.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
At home? No, not really. It's honestly just at work, which is actually why I came in to see you in the first place. At the register, I just keep getting stuck doing things that don't really fit the moment. Like, I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just completely freeze with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting for their change. It actually got me written up last week. 

My manager, Gary, handed me this formal warning on pink carbon copy paper. Do they even make carbon copy paper anymore? It seems so incredibly wasteful. Paper mills smell absolutely terrible, by the way. Have you ever driven past one? There's this distinct sulfur smell that just clings to the inside of your car for miles. Cars are getting way too complicated to fix yourself nowadays, too. Used to be you could just pop the hood and change a spark plug, but now it's all computers and microchips. The global microchip shortage really messed up the supply chain for everything, not just cars. Even basic appliances. My toaster oven broke and I had to wait three weeks for a new one to ship. I love making English muffins in the toaster oven. The nooks and crannies get so perfectly crispy if you leave them in for exactly four minutes... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Tapping? Right, no, I don't really do the tapping or freezing at home or anywhere else. Just at the store when I'm trying to ring people up.

I have to stand there and type in the twelve-digit UPC code by hand, and the buttons on my register stick sometimes. It slows down my whole line, and then the people waiting start sighing loudly and looking at their watches. \n\nWatches are kind of obsolete now anyway, aren't they? Everyone just uses their phone to check the time. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About strange ideas? Yeah, no. I don't have any weird beliefs or anything like that. Just regular thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or other sounds when there was no one around and you weren’t at work, like when you're alone in your apartment or walking by yourself?\n\nPatient: I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or telling me I rang something up wrong, but when I turn around there's nobody there. You know, they used to build things to last back in the day. The bricks they used for that factory were probably fired in a local kiln. Brickmaking used to be a huge industry in this area before everything got outsourced. Outsourcing really gutted the manufacturing sector. I was reading this article about how the rust belt got its name, and it's fascinating how entire cities just rusted away when the steel mills closed. \n\nSteel is an incredible alloy, though. Did you know they use a specific type of stainless steel for surgical instruments? It has to be completely non-porous so bacteria can't hide in it. Bacteria are everywhere, though. Like on money. Working a register, you handle so much cash, and cash is filthy. People pull crumpled, damp bills out of their socks or their bras, and I have to touch it. It's so unhygienic. I go through so much hand sanitizer on my shift that my knuckles actually crack and bleed in the winter. Winter here is brutal, the wind just whips right off the lake.\n\nBut yeah, anyway. To answer your question again, no, I don't hear them when I'm alone or walking by myself. Just at work, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself tapping or freezing in situations other than when you're at work, like when you're at home or somewhere else entirely?\n\nPatient: At home? No, not really. It's honestly just at work, which is actually why I came in to see you in the first place. At the register, I just keep getting stuck doing things that don't really fit the moment. The global microchip shortage really messed up the supply chain for everything, not just cars. Even basic appliances. My toaster oven broke and I had to wait three weeks for a new one to ship. I love making English muffins in the toaster oven. The nooks and crannies get so perfectly crispy if you leave them in for exactly four minutes... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Tapping? Right, no, I don't really do the tapping or freezing at home or anywhere else. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Embarrassed? Well... I mean, I don't really think about it like that. I guess I'm not really worried about what other people think. The customers are usually just in a hurry anyway. People are always rushing, you know? Everyone is constantly running late for something. 

I remember reading this study about how the perception of time is actually speeding up because of technology. We expect everything instantly now. Instant coffee, instant messaging. My grandmother used to percolate coffee on the stove every morning, and it took forever. It smelled incredible, though. She had this old enamel percolator, pale blue with a little glass knob on top where you could see the coffee bubbling up. Enamelware is making a huge comeback, actually. I saw a whole display of it at this antique mall I went to last month. Antique malls are fascinating, just aisles and aisles of other people's discarded memories. You can find some really strange things if you look hard enough. I found a collection of vintage glass eyes once, all lined up in a velvet-lined wooden box. 

Woodworking is a dying art, don't you think? Everything is just cheap particle board from IKEA now. You have to assemble it yourself with that tiny little hex key they give you, and it never quite lines up perfectly. 

But, uh, no, to answer your question... I don't really feel embarrassed or worried about it. I just try to do my job.

He said it woke up the beans, which doesn't make any sense, you know? But anyway, I do that, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is standing there staring at me. I don't know why every store puts the restrooms all the way in the back left corner. It's terrible floor planning. \n\nWhen I was getting my bachelor's, I took this urban design elective, and the professor—he had this really bushy mustache that covered his top lip completely, you could never actually see him smile—he said foot traffic needs to flow naturally. Like a river. Rivers don't just take sharp ninety-degree turns for no reason. Except maybe the Colorado River. I watched a documentary on PBS about that once. Like I mentioned, I'm actually on a formal warning at work now. HR handed it to me in this manila folder, but it was one of those really flimsy ones, you know? The kind that just completely folds over if you only hold it by the corner. I don't understand why corporate always cheaps out on office supplies. If you're giving someone a formal warning, it feels like it should be in a sturdy folder, or at least printed on heavy cardstock. \n\nBut even outside of my shifts... things have just kind of stopped. I've stopped keeping up with friends, and I don't even make my family calls the way I used to. My sister lives out in Oregon, so there's always that time zone math you have to do in your head before you dial. Pacific time. Daylight saving time makes it so confusing. I read once that Benjamin Franklin actually proposed daylight saving as a joke in a letter to a newspaper, but people took it seriously. People take everything so literally these days, don't they? \n\nAnyway, I haven't been keeping up with them, or my kayaking plans either. Which is a shame, honestly, because I have this beautiful yellow fiberglass kayak. Fiberglass glides through the water so much better than the cheap plastic ones, though if you hit a rock, you really feel it. The river by my house is full of these big granite rocks, and the quartz in them sparkles right around 4 PM when the sun hits the water... but yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right. I just don't do any of those things much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have persistent, unwanted thoughts or images that come into your mind that seem to be connected to these tapping patterns or freezing episodes?\n\nPatient: Thoughts or images? No, not connected to the tapping or anything. My mind is mostly just... quiet, I guess, when that happens. \n\nThough, speaking of images getting stuck in your head, lately I keep picturing this old billboard that used to be on Interstate 95. It was for a personal injury lawyer, one of those guys pointing right at you, looking really aggressive. But the paint was peeling right on the guy's nose, so it looked like he had a terrible sunburn. Sunburns are the worst, especially on the top of your shoulders. I fell asleep at the beach once back in the late nineties, just completely passed out on my towel. It was one of those oversized beach towels, the kind with a big dolphin printed on it. \n\nDolphins are incredibly smart, you know. I read they actually have names for each other. They use these specific whistles to call their friends, which is just fascinating. Imagine if people communicated like that at the store. Yeah, no, no unwanted thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to complete these tapping patterns or perform certain actions a specific way to prevent something bad from happening or to make things feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, no, it's nothing like that. It's just... I just tap. There's no reason, really. It doesn't make anything feel right or wrong. \n\nSpeaking of right and wrong, did you see they changed the layout of the cereal aisle at the grocery store? I went in there on Tuesday—or maybe it was Wednesday, because it was raining, and my umbrella has this broken spoke that always catches in my hair. It's a black umbrella with a wooden handle. I bought it at a hardware store years ago. You don't see wooden handles much anymore, do you? Everything is plastic or that rubbery material that gets sticky if you leave it in a hot car. \n\nI left a cassette tape in my car once back in, gosh, '92? It was a Fleetwood Mac album, and it completely warped in the sun. Sounded like they were singing underwater. Water always distorts sound so strangely. If you ever put your head under the bathwater, you can hear the pipes clanking in the walls. Our house had copper pipes, which I guess are valuable now because people keep stealing them out of abandoned buildings. Scrap metal prices fluctuate so much, it's basically like the stock market. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... no, the tapping isn't to stop anything bad from happening. I don't feel like I *have* to do it for any particular reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing any other repetitive actions or rituals, either physically or in your mind, that are similar to the tapping pattern in any way?\n\nPatient: Other repetitive actions? No, nothing like that. Just the tapping. \n\nThough, you know, speaking of repetitive things, they play the exact same twelve songs on a loop at the store where I work. Twelve songs. I counted them once during a slow shift. It’s always that one soft rock station, the kind that plays Phil Collins and, you know, maybe some old Elton John. Elton John has so many glasses, doesn't he? I saw a documentary once that said he owns thousands of pairs of sunglasses. I only have one pair, and they're scratched right down the middle of the left lens. I dropped them in a parking lot last winter when I was scraping ice off my windshield. \n\nIce scrapers are so poorly designed. You always end up with snow inside your sleeves, right around the wrists. It’s the worst feeling, cold wet cuffs. My grandmother used to knit these thick wool sweaters that were so warm, but they were incredibly itchy. She always used this dark green yarn that smelled faintly of mothballs. Mothballs are just pure chemicals, aren't they? I don't even know if people use them anymore, now that everyone has cedar closets. Cedar smells wonderful, like a forest after it rains. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, no other rituals or anything. Just the tapping.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any sudden, brief movements or sounds that you couldn't control, like blinking, shrugging, or throat-clearing?\n\nPatient: Sudden movements? No, I can control my blinking and all that. I don't have any twitches. I mean, I clear my throat if I have a tickle, but that's just normal. \n\nThough, speaking of tickles in the throat, the pollen has been absolutely terrible this year. Have you noticed the yellow dust on all the cars in the parking lot? My neighbor has this navy blue sedan, and it's basically green right now. Navy blue is a terrible color for showing dirt. I had a navy blue suit once, bought it for a cousin's wedding back in Ohio. It was an outdoor wedding in late October, which is a massive gamble with the weather if you ask me. \n\nIt ended up being freezing, just this biting wind, and for the dinner they served cold cucumber soup as the starter. Who serves cold soup when it's forty degrees outside? Soup should be hot. Like a really good minestrone. Minestrone always has those dark red kidney beans in it, though, and I'm not a huge fan of beans. The texture is just too mealy, kind of like a baked potato that's been left in the microwave for too long. Microwaves ruin bread, too, you know. It makes the crust all rubbery and impossible to chew. \n\nBut, uh... what did you ask? Shrugging? No, no sudden movements or sounds that I can't control.\n\nDoctor: Before you start tapping or freezing, do you ever feel a strange sensation, like a need or urge that builds up and is relieved by doing the action?\n\nPatient: A strange sensation? Like a build-up? No, I don't feel anything building up. It just happens. \n\nThough, talking about pressure building up, the espresso machine at the café next to my store has been making this awful hissing noise lately. The barista said the steam wand is clogged. Steam is so dangerous, you know. I burned my wrist on a tea kettle once years ago. I was making Earl Grey. Earl Grey always tastes a bit like perfume to me, probably because of the bergamot. Bergamot is a citrus fruit, which is strange because it doesn't look like a lemon or an orange at all. It's green and really bumpy. My aunt used to grow these bumpy decorative gourds in her garden every autumn. She'd put them in a big wooden bowl on the dining table. \n\nWooden bowls are so hard to take care of, you can't put them in the dishwasher or the heat cracks them right down the middle. My brother put a wooden cutting board in the dishwasher once and completely ruined it. He was always doing things like that, just never reading the instructions. Instructions are printed so small these days anyway, you need a magnifying glass just to read how to set the time on a microwave. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Something about an urge? No, no, I don't feel anything like that before I freeze or tap.\n\nDoctor: Before or during these tapping or freezing episodes, do you ever notice any unusual sensory experiences, like strange smells, sounds, or things you see that don't seem to be there?\n\nPatient: Unusual sensory things? Well, I don't know about smells or seeing things, but... well, most days I hear what sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or saying I rang something up wrong. But when I turn around, there's nobody there, and the other cashiers never hear it. \n\nSpeaking of ringing things up wrong, the new point-of-sale system they installed last month is just a nightmare. The touch screen is completely unresponsive if your fingers are even slightly cold. My hands are always cold, poor circulation, I suppose. My grandfather had terrible circulation, he used to wear these thick wool socks even in the middle of July. July in July is unbearable, especially with the humidity. It makes the air feel thick, like you're trying to breathe underwater. I tried scuba diving once on a vacation to Florida back in the nineties. \n\nFlorida is so flat, isn't it? Just miles and miles of strip malls and palm trees. Palm trees aren't actually trees, you know, they're more closely related to grass. It's fascinating how taxonomy works, grouping things together based on cellular structures instead of what they look like. Like how a tomato is a fruit, but everyone treats it like a vegetable. You'd never put a tomato in a fruit salad, that would be disgusting. Fruit salad always has honeydew melon, which is just filler if you ask me. Nobody actually likes honeydew. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right. The voices. Yeah, just those people talking when no one's there. No strange smells or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these tapping patterns or freezing episodes happen only when you're at work, or do they also occur in other situations, like when you're at home or out running errands?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly at work. That's actually why I came in, because at the register I keep getting stuck doing these things that just don't fit the moment. Like, I'll be ringing someone up and I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. It's getting really bad. Last week, it actually got me written up by the manager. \n\nManagers can be so unreasonable, though. He wrote it on this pink slip of paper. Why do they still use pink slips? It feels so outdated, like something out of a fifties movie. I love old movies, especially the ones with Cary Grant. He always wore those incredible tailored suits. Tailoring is a lost art, really. Nowadays, everyone just buys off the rack and expects it to fit perfectly, but human bodies aren't built on an assembly line. \n\nAssembly lines completely changed the automotive industry, Henry Ford and all that. My dad had an old Ford pickup truck when I was a kid. It was this faded cherry red, and the vinyl seats would get so hot in the summer they'd practically blister your legs if you wore shorts. I don't wear shorts anymore, not since I turned forty. I just feel like grown men shouldn't wear shorts unless they're at the beach. The beach is getting so crowded these days, though. You can't even find a spot to put down a towel without being right on top of someone else's umbrella. Umbrellas are tricky in the wind, they always turn inside out. \n\nBut... wait. What were you asking me about? Errands? Oh, no, the freezing and tapping... it really just happens when I'm at the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear these voices calling your name or talking to you only when you're at work or during these tapping or freezing episodes, or do you hear them at other times too, like when you're at home or alone?\n\nPatient: Well, the voices... I hear them mostly at work, like I said before. It's usually when I'm near the stockroom, just off to the side. They'll call my name or tell me I messed up ringing something up. But I don't really hear them at home. Home is pretty quiet. \n\nToo quiet, sometimes. I live in this apartment building that used to be an old shoe factory. They converted it back in the early two-thousands. Exposed brick is nice to look at, but it’s terrible for insulation. In the winter, the draft just cuts right through the walls. I bought this heavy velvet curtain to put over the window, thinking it would help block the cold. Velvet is such a strange fabric, isn't it? It catches the dust like crazy. I feel like I'm constantly running the lint roller over it. Lint rollers are basically just giant rolls of tape on a stick. \n\nTape is one of those inventions you don't really think about until you need it and can't find the end of the roll. My dad used to get so frustrated with duct tape, he'd just take a pocket knife and slash a new edge into it. He carried that pocket knife everywhere. It had a little bone handle that was yellowed from age. He said he got it at a hardware store in Chicago when he was a teenager. \n\nChicago is famous for deep-dish pizza, but honestly, I think it's more of a casserole than a pizza. If you have to eat it with a knife and fork, it defeats the purpose of pizza. Pizza should be something you can fold in half and eat while walking down the street in New York. New York always seems so exhausting, though. Everyone is always rushing somewhere. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About the voices? Yeah, like I said, it's just those people talking off to the side, mostly at work. They call my name or say I rang something wrong, but no one's there. I don't really hear them at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or noticed any other unusual sounds or sensations when you're not at work or when you're alone, even when there's no one around to explain it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's mostly at work, you know? Most days I hear these couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or telling me I rang something up wrong. But when I turn around to look, there's nobody there, and the other cashiers never hear a thing. \n\nI don't really hear them when I'm alone at home, though. Home is... well, it's quiet. I have this old grandfather clock in the hallway, and sometimes the ticking is the only sound in the whole apartment. It belonged to my great-aunt Mildred. She was a bit eccentric, used to collect porcelain thimbles. She had hundreds of them, all lined up in these little wooden display cases. I never understood the appeal of collecting things you can't even use. It's just dusting, really. Dusting is such a chore. I tried using one of those microfiber wands, but it just seems to push the dust around instead of picking it up. \n\nMicrofiber is a strange material. It feels weird on your skin, almost like it's catching on your fingerprints. I read somewhere that criminals used to burn off their fingerprints with acid so they wouldn't get caught. Can you imagine the pain? Acid is terrifying. I remember in high school chemistry, Mr. Henderson dropped a beaker of hydrochloric acid and it ate right through the linoleum floor. Linoleum is making a comeback, you know. People are putting it in their kitchens again because it's eco-friendly. \n\nEco-friendly is such a buzzword now. Every product claims to be green, but half of it is just marketing. Like those paper straws—they dissolve in your drink before you're even halfway done! I'd rather just not use a straw at all. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? About the sounds? Yeah, just the voices at work, like I said. Calling my name or talking about the register. But not when I'm alone or at home. Just at the store.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people don't see, like shadows, people, or objects that aren't actually there?\n\nPatient: See things? No, no shadows or anything like that. Like I keep telling you, it's really just the sounds. Most days I just hear what sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or saying I rang something up wrong, but when I turn around there’s nobody there and no one else heard it. \n\nI turned around so fast the other day I actually gave myself a terrible crick in my neck. I thought about going to a chiropractor, but I've seen those videos online where they crack your neck and it sounds like a dry branch snapping. Snapping branches always reminds me of camping. My uncle used to take me camping up in the Adirondacks when I was a kid. We'd have to gather kindling for the fire before it got dark. Fire is so mesmerizing, isn't it? You can just stare into a campfire for hours and not even realize how much time has passed. \n\nTime is funny like that. Staring at screens does the same thing to people nowadays, though. Everyone is always just looking down at their phones, completely ignoring the world around them. My phone battery dies so fast lately, I swear they plan it that way so you have to buy a new one every two years. Planned obsolescence, it's a real economic strategy they teach you in business school. I took an economics class once when I was getting my bachelor's, but the professor just droned on and on. He wore these elbow patches on his tweed jackets. I always wondered if the elbows were actually worn out, or if he bought the jacket with the patches already sewn on. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Shadows? Oh, right. No, I don't see anything that isn't there. Just the people talking at the store, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people seem to think are strange or untrue, but you feel certain they are real?\n\nPatient: Strange beliefs? No, I don't think so. I'm a pretty grounded guy. I mean, I read a lot of history, and history is full of people with strange ideas. Like, did you know people used to think tomatoes were poisonous? They called them \"poison apples\" back in the 1700s because aristocrats would eat them off pewter plates, and the acid in the tomato would leach the lead out of the plate and give them lead poisoning. \n\nLead is so incredibly dangerous, but they used to put it in everything. Paint, gasoline. My apartment building—the old shoe factory I mentioned—when they converted it, they had to do a massive lead abatement before anyone could move in. The contractors were walking around in these full-body white suits, looking like astronauts. \n\nSpace travel just seems absolutely terrifying to me, to be honest. Being stuck in a tiny tin can millions of miles from earth with no fresh air. And the food! It's all freeze-dried and vacuum-sealed. I can't stand vacuum-sealed packaging. Sometimes at the store, customers bring up those vacuum-sealed steaks, and the plastic gets all crinkled up around the barcode so the laser scanner won't read it. I have to stand there and type in the twelve-digit UPC code by hand, and the buttons on my register stick sometimes. It slows down my whole line, and then the people waiting start sighing loudly and looking at their watches. \n\nWatches are kind of obsolete now anyway, aren't they? Everyone just uses their phone to check the time. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About strange ideas? Yeah, no. I don't have any weird beliefs or anything like that. Just regular thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or other sounds when there was no one around and you weren’t at work, like when you're alone in your apartment or walking by yourself?\n\nPatient: I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or telling me I rang something up wrong, but when I turn around there's nobody there. Brickmaking used to be a huge industry in this area before everything got outsourced. Outsourcing really gutted the manufacturing sector. I was reading this article about how the rust belt got its name, and it's fascinating how entire cities just rusted away when the steel mills closed. \n\nSteel is an incredible alloy, though. Did you know they use a specific type of stainless steel for surgical instruments? It has to be completely non-porous so bacteria can't hide in it. Bacteria are everywhere, though. Like on money. Working a register, you handle so much cash, and cash is filthy. People pull crumpled, damp bills out of their socks or their bras, and I have to touch it. It's so unhygienic. I go through so much hand sanitizer on my shift that my knuckles actually crack and bleed in the winter. Winter here is brutal, the wind just whips right off the lake.\n\nBut yeah, anyway. Cars are getting way too complicated to fix yourself nowadays, too. Used to be you could just pop the hood and change a spark plug, but now it's all computers and microchips. The global microchip shortage really messed up the supply chain for everything, not just cars. Even basic appliances. My toaster oven broke and I had to wait three weeks for a new one to ship. I love making English muffins in the toaster oven. The nooks and crannies get so perfectly crispy if you leave them in for exactly four minutes... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Tapping? Right, no, I don't really do the tapping or freezing at home or anywhere else. We expect everything instantly now. Instant coffee, instant messaging. My grandmother used to percolate coffee on the stove every morning, and it took forever. It smelled incredible, though. I don't really feel embarrassed or worried about it. I just try to do my job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. New York always seems so exhausting, though. Everyone is always rushing somewhere. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About the voices? I don't really hear them at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or noticed any other unusual sounds or sensations when you're not at work or when you're alone, even when there's no one around to explain it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's mostly at work, you know? Most days I hear these couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or telling me I rang something up wrong. But when I turn around to look, there's nobody there, and the other cashiers never hear a thing. \n\nI don't really hear them when I'm alone at home, though. Home is... well, it's quiet. I have this old grandfather clock in the hallway, and sometimes the ticking is the only sound in the whole apartment. It belonged to my great-aunt Mildred. She was a bit eccentric, used to collect porcelain thimbles. She had hundreds of them, all lined up in these little wooden display cases. I never understood the appeal of collecting things you can't even use. It's just dusting, really. Dusting is such a chore. I tried using one of those microfiber wands, but it just seems to push the dust around instead of picking it up. \n\nMicrofiber is a strange material. It feels weird on your skin, almost like it's catching on your fingerprints. I read somewhere that criminals used to burn off their fingerprints with acid so they wouldn't get caught. Can you imagine the pain? Acid is terrifying. I remember in high school chemistry, Mr. Henderson dropped a beaker of hydrochloric acid and it ate right through the linoleum floor. Linoleum is making a comeback, you know. People are putting it in their kitchens again because it's eco-friendly. \n\nEco-friendly is such a buzzword now. Every product claims to be green, but half of it is just marketing. Like those paper straws—they dissolve in your drink before you're even halfway done! I'd rather just not use a straw at all. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? About the sounds? Yeah, just the voices at work, like I said. Calling my name or talking about the register. But not when I'm alone or at home. Just at the store.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people don't see, like shadows, people, or objects that aren't actually there?\n\nPatient: See things? No, no shadows or anything like that. Like I keep telling you, it's really just the sounds. Most days I just hear what sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or saying I rang something up wrong, but when I turn around there’s nobody there and no one else heard it. \n\nI turned around so fast the other day I actually gave myself a terrible crick in my neck. I thought about going to a chiropractor, but I've seen those videos online where they crack your neck and it sounds like a dry branch snapping. Snapping branches always reminds me of camping. My uncle used to take me camping up in the Adirondacks when I was a kid. We'd have to gather kindling for the fire before it got dark. Fire is so mesmerizing, isn't it? You can just stare into a campfire for hours and not even realize how much time has passed. \n\nTime is funny like that. Staring at screens does the same thing to people nowadays, though. Everyone is always just looking down at their phones, completely ignoring the world around them. My phone battery dies so fast lately, I swear they plan it that way so you have to buy a new one every two years. Planned obsolescence, it's a real economic strategy they teach you in business school. I took an economics class once when I was getting my bachelor's, but the professor just droned on and on. He wore these elbow patches on his tweed jackets. I always wondered if the elbows were actually worn out, or if he bought the jacket with the patches already sewn on. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Shadows? Oh, right. No, I don't see anything that isn't there. Just the people talking at the store, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that other people seem to think are strange or untrue, but you feel certain they are real?\n\nPatient: Strange beliefs? No, I don't think so. I'm a pretty grounded guy. I mean, I read a lot of history, and history is full of people with strange ideas. Like, did you know people used to think tomatoes were poisonous? They called them \"poison apples\" back in the 1700s because aristocrats would eat them off pewter plates, and the acid in the tomato would leach the lead out of the plate and give them lead poisoning. \n\nLead is so incredibly dangerous, but they used to put it in everything. Paint, gasoline. My apartment building—the old shoe factory I mentioned—when they converted it, they had to do a massive lead abatement before anyone could move in. The contractors were walking around in these full-body white suits, looking like astronauts. \n\nSpace travel just seems absolutely terrifying to me, to be honest. Being stuck in a tiny tin can millions of miles from earth with no fresh air. And the food! It's all freeze-dried and vacuum-sealed. I can't stand vacuum-sealed packaging. Sometimes at the store, customers bring up those vacuum-sealed steaks, and the plastic gets all crinkled up around the barcode so the laser scanner won't read it. I have to stand there and type in the twelve-digit UPC code by hand, and the buttons on my register stick sometimes. It slows down my whole line, and then the people waiting start sighing loudly and looking at their watches. \n\nWatches are kind of obsolete now anyway, aren't they? Everyone just uses their phone to check the time. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About strange ideas? Yeah, no. I don't have any weird beliefs or anything like that. Just regular thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or other sounds when there was no one around and you weren’t at work, like when you're alone in your apartment or walking by yourself?\n\nPatient: I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like I was saying before, it's just at the store. Most days I'll be ringing people up and I hear these couple of people talking, just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or telling me I rang something up wrong, but when I turn around there's nobody there. And none of the other cashiers ever hear it. \n\nI really don't hear them when I'm alone in my apartment. My apartment is in that old shoe factory, remember? The walls are incredibly thick. You know, they used to build things to last back in the day. The bricks they used for that factory were probably fired in a local kiln. Brickmaking used to be a huge industry in this area before everything got outsourced. Outsourcing really gutted the manufacturing sector. I was reading this article about how the rust belt got its name, and it's fascinating how entire cities just rusted away when the steel mills closed. \n\nSteel is an incredible alloy, though. Did you know they use a specific type of stainless steel for surgical instruments? It has to be completely non-porous so bacteria can't hide in it. Bacteria are everywhere, though. Like on money. Working a register, you handle so much cash, and cash is filthy. People pull crumpled, damp bills out of their socks or their bras, and I have to touch it. It's so unhygienic. I go through so much hand sanitizer on my shift that my knuckles actually crack and bleed in the winter. Winter here is brutal, the wind just whips right off the lake.\n\nBut yeah, anyway. To answer your question again, no, I don't hear them when I'm alone or walking by myself. Just at work, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself tapping or freezing in situations other than when you're at work, like when you're at home or somewhere else entirely?\n\nPatient: At home? No, not really. It's honestly just at work, which is actually why I came in to see you in the first place. At the register, I just keep getting stuck doing things that don't really fit the moment. Like, I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern, or I'll just completely freeze with my hand hovering right over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting for their change. It actually got me written up last week. \n\nMy manager, Gary, handed me this formal warning on pink carbon copy paper. Do they even make carbon copy paper anymore? It seems so incredibly wasteful. Paper mills smell absolutely terrible, by the way. Have you ever driven past one? There's this distinct sulfur smell that just clings to the inside of your car for miles. Cars are getting way too complicated to fix yourself nowadays, too. Used to be you could just pop the hood and change a spark plug, but now it's all computers and microchips. The global microchip shortage really messed up the supply chain for everything, not just cars. Even basic appliances. My toaster oven broke and I had to wait three weeks for a new one to ship. I love making English muffins in the toaster oven. The nooks and crannies get so perfectly crispy if you leave them in for exactly four minutes... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Tapping? Right, no, I don't really do the tapping or freezing at home or anywhere else. Just at the store when I'm trying to ring people up.\n\nDoctor: Do these experiences, like hearing voices or freezing up at work, ever make you feel embarrassed, worried, or concerned about what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? Well... I mean, I don't really think about it like that. I guess I'm not really worried about what other people think. The customers are usually just in a hurry anyway. People are always rushing, you know? Everyone is constantly running late for something. \n\nI remember reading this study about how the perception of time is actually speeding up because of technology. We expect everything instantly now. Instant coffee, instant messaging. My grandmother used to percolate coffee on the stove every morning, and it took forever. It smelled incredible, though. She had this old enamel percolator, pale blue with a little glass knob on top where you could see the coffee bubbling up. Enamelware is making a huge comeback, actually. I saw a whole display of it at this antique mall I went to last month. Antique malls are fascinating, just aisles and aisles of other people's discarded memories. You can find some really strange things if you look hard enough. I found a collection of vintage glass eyes once, all lined up in a velvet-lined wooden box. \n\nWoodworking is a dying art, don't you think? Everything is just cheap particle board from IKEA now. You have to assemble it yourself with that tiny little hex key they give you, and it never quite lines up perfectly. \n\nBut, uh, no, to answer your question... I don't really feel embarrassed or worried about it. I just try to do my job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Thoughts? Like... strange beliefs? No, I don't think so. I mean, I just see things the way they are. Everyone has their own perspective, right? Perspective is so important in art, especially in Renaissance paintings. They really mastered the vanishing point. Before that, everything in medieval art looked so flat and two-dimensional, like all the figures were just pasted on top of each other. 

I actually took an art history class in college. It was an elective, but it ended up being my favorite course. The professor had this enormous collection of slide carousels. The click-clack sound those projectors make is so nostalgic. It's a shame everything is digital now. Digital projectors are convenient, but the bulbs burn out so fast and they cost a fortune to replace. I tried to replace a bulb in my porch light last week and the whole fixture was rusted shut. Rust is just oxidation, iron reacting with water and oxygen. It's a slow burn, really. 

Fire is fascinating, isn't it? How it just consumes whatever is in its path. Controlled burns are actually really good for forests, though. They clear out the dead underbrush so new seeds can sprout. Pinecones, for example, some species actually need the heat of a forest fire to open up and release their seeds. Nature has such incredible mechanisms for survival. 

But, uh, anyway. No, I don't have any unusual thoughts or anything like that. Just the normal stuff.

But anyway, I do that, or I'll just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer while the customer is standing there staring at me. And last week, my manager actually wrote me up for it. He used this blue pen, which I hate, blue ink always looks so unprofessional on official forms, don't you think? It should be black ink. Black ink means business. But yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't complete these tapping patterns or if something interrupts you?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I haven't really noticed feeling upset or anything. If someone interrupts me, I just... stop, I guess. People interrupt all the time anyway, usually to ask where the restrooms are. I don't know why every store puts the restrooms all the way in the back left corner. It's terrible floor planning. \n\nWhen I was getting my bachelor's, I took this urban design elective, and the professor—he had this really bushy mustache that covered his top lip completely, you could never actually see him smile—he said foot traffic needs to flow naturally. Like a river. Rivers don't just take sharp ninety-degree turns for no reason. Except maybe the Colorado River. I watched a documentary on PBS about that once. The water just carving through the red rock over millions of years... it's really incredible when you think about it. \n\nBut, uh... no, I don't get anxious if I can't finish the tapping.\n\nDoctor: Do these tapping patterns or freezing episodes ever cause you distress, or make it hard for you to do your job or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely makes the job hard, yeah. Like I mentioned, I'm actually on a formal warning at work now. HR handed it to me in this manila folder, but it was one of those really flimsy ones, you know? The kind that just completely folds over if you only hold it by the corner. I don't understand why corporate always cheaps out on office supplies. If you're giving someone a formal warning, it feels like it should be in a sturdy folder, or at least printed on heavy cardstock. \n\nBut even outside of my shifts... things have just kind of stopped. I've stopped keeping up with friends, and I don't even make my family calls the way I used to. My sister lives out in Oregon, so there's always that time zone math you have to do in your head before you dial. Pacific time. Daylight saving time makes it so confusing. I read once that Benjamin Franklin actually proposed daylight saving as a joke in a letter to a newspaper, but people took it seriously. People take everything so literally these days, don't they? \n\nAnyway, I haven't been keeping up with them, or my kayaking plans either. Which is a shame, honestly, because I have this beautiful yellow fiberglass kayak. Fiberglass glides through the water so much better than the cheap plastic ones, though if you hit a rock, you really feel it. The river by my house is full of these big granite rocks, and the quartz in them sparkles right around 4 PM when the sun hits the water... but yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right. I just don't do any of those things much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have persistent, unwanted thoughts or images that come into your mind that seem to be connected to these tapping patterns or freezing episodes?\n\nPatient: Thoughts or images? No, not connected to the tapping or anything. My mind is mostly just... quiet, I guess, when that happens. \n\nThough, speaking of images getting stuck in your head, lately I keep picturing this old billboard that used to be on Interstate 95. It was for a personal injury lawyer, one of those guys pointing right at you, looking really aggressive. But the paint was peeling right on the guy's nose, so it looked like he had a terrible sunburn. Sunburns are the worst, especially on the top of your shoulders. I fell asleep at the beach once back in the late nineties, just completely passed out on my towel. It was one of those oversized beach towels, the kind with a big dolphin printed on it. \n\nDolphins are incredibly smart, you know. I read they actually have names for each other. They use these specific whistles to call their friends, which is just fascinating. Imagine if people communicated like that at the store. Just whistling over the intercom instead of calling for a price check on aisle four. The intercom at my store has this awful static buzz anyway. It sounds like a hornet's nest every time someone presses the button. But, uh... what was the question? Something about the tapping? Yeah, no, no unwanted thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to complete these tapping patterns or perform certain actions a specific way to prevent something bad from happening or to make things feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, no, it's nothing like that. It's just... I just tap. There's no reason, really. It doesn't make anything feel right or wrong. \n\nSpeaking of right and wrong, did you see they changed the layout of the cereal aisle at the grocery store? I went in there on Tuesday—or maybe it was Wednesday, because it was raining, and my umbrella has this broken spoke that always catches in my hair. It's a black umbrella with a wooden handle. I bought it at a hardware store years ago. You don't see wooden handles much anymore, do you? Everything is plastic or that rubbery material that gets sticky if you leave it in a hot car. \n\nI left a cassette tape in my car once back in, gosh, '92? It was a Fleetwood Mac album, and it completely warped in the sun. Sounded like they were singing underwater. Water always distorts sound so strangely. If you ever put your head under the bathwater, you can hear the pipes clanking in the walls. Our house had copper pipes, which I guess are valuable now because people keep stealing them out of abandoned buildings. Scrap metal prices fluctuate so much, it's basically like the stock market. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... no, the tapping isn't to stop anything bad from happening. I don't feel like I *have* to do it for any particular reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing any other repetitive actions or rituals, either physically or in your mind, that are similar to the tapping pattern in any way?\n\nPatient: Other repetitive actions? No, nothing like that. Just the tapping. \n\nThough, you know, speaking of repetitive things, they play the exact same twelve songs on a loop at the store where I work. Twelve songs. I counted them once during a slow shift. It’s always that one soft rock station, the kind that plays Phil Collins and, you know, maybe some old Elton John. Elton John has so many glasses, doesn't he? I saw a documentary once that said he owns thousands of pairs of sunglasses. I only have one pair, and they're scratched right down the middle of the left lens. I dropped them in a parking lot last winter when I was scraping ice off my windshield. \n\nIce scrapers are so poorly designed. You always end up with snow inside your sleeves, right around the wrists. It’s the worst feeling, cold wet cuffs. My grandmother used to knit these thick wool sweaters that were so warm, but they were incredibly itchy. She always used this dark green yarn that smelled faintly of mothballs. Mothballs are just pure chemicals, aren't they? I don't even know if people use them anymore, now that everyone has cedar closets. Cedar smells wonderful, like a forest after it rains. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, no other rituals or anything. Just the tapping.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any sudden, brief movements or sounds that you couldn't control, like blinking, shrugging, or throat-clearing?\n\nPatient: Sudden movements? No, I can control my blinking and all that. I don't have any twitches. I mean, I clear my throat if I have a tickle, but that's just normal. \n\nThough, speaking of tickles in the throat, the pollen has been absolutely terrible this year. Have you noticed the yellow dust on all the cars in the parking lot? My neighbor has this navy blue sedan, and it's basically green right now. Navy blue is a terrible color for showing dirt. I had a navy blue suit once, bought it for a cousin's wedding back in Ohio. It was an outdoor wedding in late October, which is a massive gamble with the weather if you ask me. \n\nIt ended up being freezing, just this biting wind, and for the dinner they served cold cucumber soup as the starter. Who serves cold soup when it's forty degrees outside? Soup should be hot. Like a really good minestrone. Minestrone always has those dark red kidney beans in it, though, and I'm not a huge fan of beans. The texture is just too mealy, kind of like a baked potato that's been left in the microwave for too long. Microwaves ruin bread, too, you know. It makes the crust all rubbery and impossible to chew. \n\nBut, uh... what did you ask? Shrugging? No, no sudden movements or sounds that I can't control.\n\nDoctor: Before you start tapping or freezing, do you ever feel a strange sensation, like a need or urge that builds up and is relieved by doing the action?\n\nPatient: A strange sensation? Like a build-up? No, I don't feel anything building up. It just happens. \n\nThough, talking about pressure building up, the espresso machine at the café next to my store has been making this awful hissing noise lately. The barista said the steam wand is clogged. Steam is so dangerous, you know. I burned my wrist on a tea kettle once years ago. I was making Earl Grey. Earl Grey always tastes a bit like perfume to me, probably because of the bergamot. Bergamot is a citrus fruit, which is strange because it doesn't look like a lemon or an orange at all. It's green and really bumpy. My aunt used to grow these bumpy decorative gourds in her garden every autumn. She'd put them in a big wooden bowl on the dining table. \n\nWooden bowls are so hard to take care of, you can't put them in the dishwasher or the heat cracks them right down the middle. My brother put a wooden cutting board in the dishwasher once and completely ruined it. He was always doing things like that, just never reading the instructions. Instructions are printed so small these days anyway, you need a magnifying glass just to read how to set the time on a microwave. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Something about an urge? No, no, I don't feel anything like that before I freeze or tap.\n\nDoctor: Before or during these tapping or freezing episodes, do you ever notice any unusual sensory experiences, like strange smells, sounds, or things you see that don't seem to be there?\n\nPatient: Unusual sensory things? Well, I don't know about smells or seeing things, but... well, most days I hear what sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they're calling my name from the stockroom, or saying I rang something up wrong. But when I turn around, there's nobody there, and the other cashiers never hear it. \n\nSpeaking of ringing things up wrong, the new point-of-sale system they installed last month is just a nightmare. The touch screen is completely unresponsive if your fingers are even slightly cold. My hands are always cold, poor circulation, I suppose. My grandfather had terrible circulation, he used to wear these thick wool socks even in the middle of July. July in July is unbearable, especially with the humidity. It makes the air feel thick, like you're trying to breathe underwater. I tried scuba diving once on a vacation to Florida back in the nineties. \n\nFlorida is so flat, isn't it? Just miles and miles of strip malls and palm trees. Palm trees aren't actually trees, you know, they're more closely related to grass. It's fascinating how taxonomy works, grouping things together based on cellular structures instead of what they look like. Like how a tomato is a fruit, but everyone treats it like a vegetable. You'd never put a tomato in a fruit salad, that would be disgusting. Fruit salad always has honeydew melon, which is just filler if you ask me. No strange smells or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these tapping patterns or freezing episodes happen only when you're at work, or do they also occur in other situations, like when you're at home or out running errands?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's mostly at work. That's actually why I came in, because at the register I keep getting stuck doing these things that just don't fit the moment. It's getting really bad. Last week, it actually got me written up by the manager. \n\nManagers can be so unreasonable, though. He wrote it on this pink slip of paper. Why do they still use pink slips? It feels so outdated, like something out of a fifties movie. I love old movies, especially the ones with Cary Grant. He always wore those incredible tailored suits. Tailoring is a lost art, really. Nowadays, everyone just buys off the rack and expects it to fit perfectly, but human bodies aren't built on an assembly line. \n\nAssembly lines completely changed the automotive industry, Henry Ford and all that. My dad had an old Ford pickup truck when I was a kid. It was this faded cherry red, and the vinyl seats would get so hot in the summer they'd practically blister your legs if you wore shorts. I don't wear shorts anymore, not since I turned forty. I just feel like grown men shouldn't wear shorts unless they're at the beach. The beach is getting so crowded these days, though. You can't even find a spot to put down a towel without being right on top of someone else's umbrella. Umbrellas are tricky in the wind, they always turn inside out. \n\nBut... wait. They converted it back in the early two-thousands. Exposed brick is nice to look at, but it’s terrible for insulation. In the winter, the draft just cuts right through the walls. I bought this heavy velvet curtain to put over the window, thinking it would help block the cold. Velvet is such a strange fabric, isn't it? It catches the dust like crazy. I feel like I'm constantly running the lint roller over it. Lint rollers are basically just giant rolls of tape on a stick. \n\nTape is one of those inventions you don't really think about until you need it and can't find the end of the roll. My dad used to get so frustrated with duct tape, he'd just take a pocket knife and slash a new edge into it. He carried that pocket knife everywhere. It had a little bone handle that was yellowed from age. He said he got it at a hardware store in Chicago when he was a teenager. \n\nChicago is famous for deep-dish pizza, but honestly, I think it's more of a casserole than a pizza. If you have to eat it with a knife and fork, it defeats the purpose of pizza. Pizza should be something you can fold in half and eat while walking down the street in New York. New York always seems so exhausting, though. Everyone is always rushing somewhere. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? About the voices? Yeah, like I said, it's just those people talking off to the side, mostly at work. They call my name or say I rang something wrong, but no one's there. Linoleum is making a comeback, you know. People are putting it in their kitchens again because it's eco-friendly. \n\nEco-friendly is such a buzzword now. Every product claims to be green, but half of it is just marketing. Like those paper straws—they dissolve in your drink before you're even halfway done! Everyone is always just looking down at their phones, completely ignoring the world around them. My phone battery dies so fast lately, I swear they plan it that way so you have to buy a new one every two years. Planned obsolescence, it's a real economic strategy they teach you in business school. I took an economics class once when I was getting my bachelor's, but the professor just droned on and on. Right, no, I don't really do the tapping or freezing at home or anywhere else. We expect everything instantly now. Instant coffee, instant messaging. My grandmother used to percolate coffee on the stove every morning, and it took forever. It smelled incredible, though. She had this old enamel percolator, pale blue with a little glass knob on top where you could see the coffee bubbling up. Enamelware is making a huge comeback, actually. I saw a whole display of it at this antique mall I went to last month. Antique malls are fascinating, just aisles and aisles of other people's discarded memories.
